A construction of tame sheaves and tame de Rham–Witt cohomology Thanks: Funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) TRR 326 Geometry and Arithmetic of Uniformized Structures , project number 444845124./
Alberto Merici, Kay Rülling and Shuji Saito

Abstract
. In this article, we consider an algebraic version of the tame site of a pair ( X , X ~ ) (X,\tilde{X}) . With this definition, we provide a general machinery to construct a tame sheaf from the data of an étale sheaf on X X and a family of local tame sections. We apply this construction to the big de Rham–Witt sheaves with tame sections defined by log poles and, over a field, to reciprocity sheaves, and deduce some consequences. As an application, we compare tame syntomic cohomology with the Nygaard filtration on the tame de Rham–Witt complex.



Introduction

Let
k
be a field and
\operatorname{\mathrm{Sm}}_{k}
be the category of smooth schemes separated and of finite type over
k
.
If
k
has characteristic zero, a well behaved cohomology theory on
\operatorname{\mathrm{Sm}}_{k}
is given by

X\mapsto R\Gamma(\overline{X},\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{X}}(\log D)),

where
\overline{X}
is a smooth compactification of
X
with
D=\overline{X}-X
a divisor with simple normal crossings and

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{X}}(\log D)
is the associated de Rham complex with logarithmic poles along
D
.
It is independent of the choice of
\overline{X}
, by
[
Del71
]
.
An important feature of (
Introduction
) is that it satisfies étale descent: For example, this follows from the comparison with singular cohomology assuming
k=\mathbf{C}
by the Lefschetz principle.

If the characteristic of
k
is
p>0
, then one can construct an integral
p
-adic cohomology theory on
\operatorname{\mathrm{Sm}}_{k}
following the above construction using the de Rham–Witt complex with log poles instead, see e.g.
[
ESS21
]
.
But it turns out that it does not satisfies étale descent.
Indeed, assuming the existence of good compactifications, the cohomology theory

X\mapsto R\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{m}R\Gamma(\overline{X},W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{X}}(\log D))

compares with rigid cohomology while the cohomology groups are finitely generated
W(k)
-modules. Therefore,
it cannot have étale descent, by
[
AC21
]
.
A counterexample is given by an Artin–Schreier covering so this discrepancy is due to wild ramification.
On the other hand, in
[
Mer25
]
, it has been shown that (under the assumption of resolutions of singularities) the cohomology theory (
Introduction
) satisfies the tame descent in the sense of
[
HS20
]
, using tools from motivic homotopy theory of logarithmic schemes. This suggests that in order to construct integral models of
p
-adic cohomology theories
of (not necessarily proper) schemes, one needs to take into account this weaker descent instead of étale descent.

The main goal of this paper is to
introduce a variant of the tame topology of
[
HS20
]
and establish its foundational results, which enable us to construct various cohomology theories which satisfy tame descent.
For a quasi-compact open immersion
S\hookrightarrow\widetilde{S}
1
1

1

For example,
S=\widetilde{S}=\operatorname{Spec}(k)
or
(S,\widetilde{S})=(\operatorname{Spec}({\operatorname{Frac}}(R)),\operatorname{Spec}(R))
for a discrete valuation ring
R
.
, we consider a tame site
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S}),t}
whose underlying category is a category of quasi-compact open immersions
X\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
over
S\hookrightarrow\widetilde{S}
with
X
of finite type over
S
and
\tilde{X}\to\widetilde{S}
satisfying a certain finiteness condition. For
(X,\tilde{X})\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S}),t}
, we also consider the tame site
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
on the category of the objects
(U,\tilde{U})
over
(X,\tilde{X})
with
U\to X
étale endowed with the induced topology of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S}),t}
.
A useful property of tame cohomology is the independence of compactifications:
For two objects
(X,\tilde{X}),\;(X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})
of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S}),t}
such that
\tilde{X},\tilde{X}^{\prime}
are both Nagata compactifications of
X
over
\widetilde{S}
, there is a natural equivalence

R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{t},F)\simeq R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{t},F),

for any sheaf
F
on
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S}),t}
, see Lemma
2.7
. Thus the formation

X\to R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{t},F)
with a choice of a compactification
\tilde{X}
gives a well-defined cohomology theory on the category of schemes separated and of finite type over
S
relative to the embedding
S\hookrightarrow\widetilde{S}
, which is functorial for morphisms of
S
-schemes.
Another useful fact is that we can provide a general machinery to construct sheaves on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
providing several examples of tame sheaves and cohomology theories satisfying the tame descent.
The construction which we call the
\beta
-construction, takes as an input an étale sheaf
F
on
X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
equipped with a local datum
\beta
of “tame sections” on every valuation ring of a residue field of
X
centered on
\tilde{X}
, and then gives as an output a tame sheaf
F_{\beta}
on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
(see Proposition
8.2
).
In the circumstances where resolution of singularities holds, it turns out that the cohomology theories (
Introduction
) and (
Introduction
) are obtained as special instances of the
\beta
-construction, giving another proof that these cohomology theories satisfy tame descent (see Theorem
10.3
and
11.3
).

The construction of the tame site
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
is similar in spirit to that of the site of
[
Hüba
]
constructed on the adic space
\mathrm{Spa}(X,\tilde{X})
. A difference here is that since the objects of our site are schemes (rather than adic spaces), it is much easier to construct sheaves on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
.

Now we state the main result on a computation of tame cohomology on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
.

Theorem 1
.

(see Theorem
7.2
):
Let
F
be a tame sheaf of abelian groups on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
such that the following condition is satisfied:

(p)

for every
(U,\tilde{U})\to(X,\tilde{X})
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, the stalk of the Zariski sheaf on
\tilde{U}
given by
\tilde{W}\subset\tilde{U}\mapsto F(\tilde{W}\cap U,\tilde{W})

at any point
x\in\tilde{U}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{(p_{x})}
-module, where

p_{x}
is the exponential characteristic of
\kappa(x)
.

Then, we have a natural equivalence

R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{t},F)\simeq\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{W}\to(X,\tilde{X})}R\Gamma(\tilde{W}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},j^{\mathcal{W}}_{*}F),

where the colimit runs along all admissible blow-ups
\mathcal{W}=(W,\tilde{W})\to(X,\tilde{X})
and
j^{\mathcal{W}}_{*}F
is the étale sheaf on
\tilde{W}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
given by
\tilde{V}/\tilde{W}\mapsto F(\tilde{V}\times_{\tilde{W}}W,\tilde{V})
.

An analogous result for torsion sheaves on adic spaces is obtained by combining
[
Hüba
, Proposition 8.5]
and
[
Hübb
, Corollary 5.7]
by a different method.
The above theorem plays a fundamental role in our subsequent article, where we construct a functorial integral structure on cohomology of the structure sheaf over a non archimedian field.

As an application, over a perfect field
k
of positive characteristics
p
, we define the
tame de Rham–Witt complex

W_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}\Omega^{\bullet,t}
, a complex of tame sheaves obtained from the de Rham–Witt complex using the
\beta
-construction mentioned above, and get the complexes

\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(r)^{t}=\operatorname{Cone}(F_{r}-\iota_{r}:\mathcal{N}^{r}W_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}\Omega^{\bullet,t}\to W_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}\Omega^{\bullet,t})[-1],

where
\mathcal{N}^{r}W_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}\Omega^{\bullet,t}
is a Nygaard filtration on
W_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}\Omega^{\bullet,t}
defined following
[
LZ04
]
(see Definition
10.6
).
Then, the
r
th
tame syntomic complex
is defined as

\mathbb{Z}_{p}(r)^{t}=R\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(r)^{t}.

We then show that the tame syntomic complex recovers the tame motivic cohomology considered in
[
Lüd24
]
and
[
Mer25
]
:

Theorem 2

(Theorem
10.8
)
.

Assume
\tilde{X}
is separated and of finite type over
k
and
X\subset\tilde{X}
is a smooth open subscheme.
We have an isomorphism of pro-complexes on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}

\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(r)^{t}=W_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}\Omega^{r,t}_{\log}[-r],

where
W_{n}\Omega^{r,t}_{\log}
is the sheaf on

(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
whose section over an object
(V,\tilde{V})
of
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
is given by

W_{n}\Omega^{r,t}_{\log}(V,\tilde{V}):=\Gamma(V,W_{n}\Omega^{r}_{X,\log}).

Using the above result we obtain a cycle map to tame de Rham–Witt cohomology.

Theorem 3

(Theorem
10.3
and Corollary
10.9
)
.

Assume
\tilde{X}
is smooth and proper over
k
and
X\subset\tilde{X}
is a dense open subscheme.
There is a functorial cycle map
{\rm cyc}^{t}
to tame de Rham–Witt cohomology which factors via tame syntomic cohomology and
is compatible with the usual crystalline cycle map
{\rm cyc}^{\rm crys}
in the sense that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{{\operatorname{CH}}^{r}(\tilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm cyc}^{\rm crys}}
\textstyle{H^{r}_{\rm crys}(\tilde{X}/W(k))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{CH}}^{r}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm cyc}^{t}}
\textstyle{H^{r}((X,\tilde{X})_{t},W\Omega^{\bullet,t}).}

Moreover, if resolution of singularities in a strong sense hold in dimension
d=\dim\tilde{X}
(this is the case for
d\leq 2
),
and if
D=\tilde{X}\setminus X
is a divisor with simple normal crossings, then there is a canonical equivalence

R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{t},W\Omega^{\bullet,t})=R\Gamma_{\rm log-crys}((\tilde{X},D)/W(k)),

where the right hand side denotes log-crystalline cohomology.

In fact using the
\beta
-construction and
[
Hes15
]

we define the tame big de Rham–Witt complex
\mathbb{W}_{T}\Omega^{\bullet,t}
on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
,
where
X\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
is any quasi-compact open immersion between qcqs schemes and
T
is any truncation set.
One of the main structure results is Proposition
9.12
which together with
Theorem
1
yields the proof of the second part of Theorem
3
.
We remark that with the same arguments and
[
Del71
]
one deduces in case
X
is smooth and separated
over
\mathbf{C}
with some compactification
\tilde{X}
the equivalence

R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X}),\Omega^{\bullet,t})\simeq R\Gamma_{\text{Betti}}(X(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}).

The following is an algebraic version of the comparison of the tame cohomology with tame adic cohomology,
in
[
HS20
, 14.8]
(there for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-schemes).

Theorem 4

(Proposition
12.2
and Theorem
12.5
)
.

Let
S
be an affine scheme and
X
an
S
-scheme with a quasi-compact open
immersion
X\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
into a proper
S
-scheme
\tilde{X}
. Then there are adjoint functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X/S)_{t}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

with
u^{*}
exact, and for all
G\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X/S)_{t})
we have an equivalence

R\Gamma((X/S)_{t},G)\simeq R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{t},u^{*}G).

As we can easily construct sheaves on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
one might wonder how the cohomology of
a sheaf
F
on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
compares to the cohomology of
u_{*}F
on
(X/S)_{t}
.
These do not coincide in general and in fact can be rather different.
For example, if
S=k
is an algebraically closed field of positive characteristic
p
,
X
is a smooth separated
k
-scheme of
dimension
d\leq 3
,
X\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
is a smooth compactification, and
\mathcal{O}^{t}
is the tame structure sheaf on
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, then

H^{i}((X,\tilde{X})_{t},\mathcal{O}^{t})=H^{i}(\tilde{X},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}})\quad\text{and}\quad H^{i}((X/k)_{t},u_{*}\mathcal{O}^{t})=H^{i}(\tilde{X}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathbb{Z}/p)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}k.

These two cohomology groups only coincide if the Frobenius acts bijectively on the cohomology of the structure sheaf.
If
\tilde{X}
is a supersingular elliptic curve and
X
is any non-empty open, then the left hand side
is a one dimensional
k
-vector space whereas the right hand side vanishes,
see Remark
12.6
.

In fact a similar phenomena also appears in characteristic zero.
Assume
{\rm ch}(k)=0
,
\tilde{X}
is smooth and proper over
k
, and
D=\tilde{X}\setminus X
has simple normal crossings.
Then we have by Theorem
3
and Lemma
12.7

H^{j}((X,\tilde{X})_{t},\Omega^{q,t})=H^{j}(\tilde{X},\Omega^{q}_{\tilde{X}}(\log{D})),

whereas

H^{j}((X/k)_{t},u_{*}\Omega^{q,t})=H^{j}(X,h^{0}_{\mathbf{A}^{1}}(\Omega^{q})),

where
h^{0}_{\mathbf{A}^{1}}(\Omega^{q})
is the maximal
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-invariant subsheaf of
\Omega^{q}
.
Here we use that
h^{0}_{\mathbf{A}^{1}}(\Omega^{q})
is in fact an étale sheaf on
X
, by
[
Voe00
, Proposition 5.24]
.
Note however that in positive characteristic it is not an étale sheaf, see Remark
12.8
for details.

Here is an outline of the paper:

(1)

In Section
1
, we give some preliminary results on not necessarily Noetherian compactifications that will be used later. This uses the notion of
ift
morphisms due to Temkin (see
[
Tem13
]
).

(2)

Sections
2
–
7
are devoted to the definition and properties of the tame and
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
site. These definitions and computations are very much inspired by the definition of the étale and tame site of a Huber pair (see
[
Hüba
]
and
[
Hüb21
]
), but with some technical difficulties in the proofs to be addressed, therefore everything needed to be rewritten:

(a)

In Section
2
and
3
, we define the tame and
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
topos and prove some immediate results.

(b)

In Section
4
and
5
we show the existence of acyclic schemes and compute the local schemes for the tame and
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
topology. The proof of Proposition
5.2
is
rather long and follows the proof of a similar computation in
[
KM
]
.

(c)

In Section
6
, we use the existence of such acyclic objects to show that Čech cohomology agrees with sheaf cohomology. This relies on the classical strategy by Artin
[
Art71
]
and is very close to
[
HS20
, Theorem 11.1 and Corollary 11.7]
.

(d)

In Section
7
we prove Theorem
1
.

(3)

In Sections
8
–
11
, we give the construction of tame sheaves and provide examples:

(a)

In Section
8
we construct the general machinery.

(b)

In Section
9
we prove basic structural results on the tame big de Rham–Witt complex.

(c)

In Section
10
, we restrict to the case of positive characteristic and compute the cohomology of the tame
p
-typical de Rham–Witt sheaves, and we deduce some results on tame syntomic cohomology.

(d)

In Section
11
, we consider reciprocity sheaves over a perfect field
k
.

(4)

Finally, in Section
12
we prove a comparison with the tame site of
[
HS20
]
, and we remark that the cohomology theories constructed in the previous sections have tame descent even though they do not come from
cohomology theories of sheaves over the site
(X/S)_{t}
. This implies that passing from the site
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
to the site
(X/S)_{t}
, a lot of information is lost.

Future work

In subsequent papers, we hope to use the construction and properties of tame sheaves developed here to construct integral models of cohomology theories in positive and mixed characteristics. In particular, for
X
smooth over a non-archimedean field
K
, the constructions above give
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-models of coherent and de Rham cohomology. According to the various result on failure of existence of integral structures on crystalline cohomology (see e.g.
[
AC21
]
), the tame descent is remarkable, as étale descent indeed cannot be fulfilled.

We thank Katharina Hübner and Amine Koubaa for useful discussions and Hugo Zock for pointing out a mistake in an earlier version.
We also thank Vincent Cossart for answering a question on resolution of singularities in positive characteristic.

1.
Ift morphisms

We recall a notion inspired by
[
Hub96
]
, and hinted in
[
Tem13
, Remark 2.2.4 (iii)]
.

Definition 1.1
.

(1)

Let
R
be a ring. A map
R\to A
is
ift
(integral over finite type) if there exists a factorization
R\to A^{\prime}\to A
with
R\to A^{\prime}
of finite type and
A^{\prime}\to A
integral.

(2)

A morphism
f\colon X\to S
is
ift at
x\in X
if there exists an affine open neighborhood
\operatorname{Spec}(A)=U\subseteq X
of
x
and an affine open
\operatorname{Spec}(R)=V\subseteq S
with
V\subseteq f(U)
such that the induced map
R\to A
is ift.

(3)

A morphism
f\colon X\to S
is
locally ift
if it is ift at all
x\in X
and it is
ift
if it is locally ift and quasi-compact.

Remark 1.2
.

As remarked in the proof of
[
Tem13
, Proposition 2.2.5]
, for
R\to R^{\prime}
integral and
R^{\prime}\to A
of finite type, the composition
R\to A
is ift. Hence, being (locally) ift is stable under compositions and if
X\to Y
is a morphism of
quasi-compact quasi-separated schemes over a base
S
with
X
locally ift over
S
, then
X\to Y
is locally ift. Moreover, it is clear that the property is stable under base change and, as observed in
[
Tem13
, Remark 2.2.4]
, it is a local property on the source,

In our context, we will consider ift morphisms that are of finite presentation on a fixed open. These morphisms will then be
nft
in the sense of
[
Tem13
, Definition 2.2.2 and Lemma 2.2.7]
, but as we will need to keep track of the fixed open, we will not use this notion. We prove the following lemma, which generalizes
[
Tem13
, Proposition 2.3.8]
:

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
\varphi:\widetilde{R}\to R
and
\psi:\tilde{A}\to A
be maps of rings and

(f,\tilde{f}):(R,\widetilde{R})\to(A,\tilde{A})
be a pair of maps of rings compatible with
\varphi
and
\psi
such that

f
is of finite presentation and
\tilde{f}
is ift.
Let
\{(B_{i},\tilde{B}_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be a filtered system of pairs of rings such that
\tilde{B}_{i}\subseteq B_{i}
are integrally closed and
(g_{i},\tilde{g}_{i})_{i\in I}\colon(R,\widetilde{R})\to\{(B_{i},\tilde{B}_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be a system of pairs of maps of rings.
Let
B=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}B_{i}
and
\tilde{B}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}\tilde{B}_{i}
and
(g,\tilde{g})=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}(g_{i},\tilde{g}_{i}):(R,\widetilde{R})\to(B,\tilde{B})
. Then, for all
(h,\tilde{h})\colon(A,\tilde{A})\to(B,\tilde{B})
compatible with
(f,\tilde{f})
and
(g,\tilde{g})
, there is
i\in I
and
(h_{i},\tilde{h}_{i})
fitting into the following commutative squares of pairs of rings:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R,\widetilde{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\tilde{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{i},\tilde{B}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\tilde{B}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\tilde{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{i},\tilde{g}_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h,\tilde{h})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{i},\tilde{h}_{i})}

If
\tilde{g}_{i}
are ift, then so is
\tilde{h}_{i}
.

Proof.

As
f\colon R\to A
is of finite presentation,
h\colon A\to B
factors through
h_{i}\colon A\to B_{i}
for some
i
by
[
Sta16
,
Tag 00QO
]
. We need to show that the composition
\tilde{A}\to A\xrightarrow{h_{i}}B_{i}
factors via

\tilde{B}_{i}\hookrightarrow B_{i}
for
i
large enough.
By definition,
\tilde{f}\colon\widetilde{R}\to\tilde{A}
factors as a composition
\widetilde{R}\to\widetilde{R}[t_{1}\ldots t_{n}]\twoheadrightarrow E\xrightarrow{\varphi}\tilde{A}
with
\varphi
integral. As
\widetilde{R}\to\widetilde{R}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
is of finite presentation, for
i
large enough, there is a map
\nu\colon\widetilde{R}[t_{1},\ldots t_{n}]\to\tilde{B}_{i}

fitting in the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\tilde{R}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{\tilde{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{i}}
\textstyle{\tilde{B}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{i}}

Since the composite
\tilde{R}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\to A
factors through
E
, the kernel of
\widetilde{R}[x_{1},\ldots x_{n}]\twoheadrightarrow E
maps to zero in
B_{i}
via this composition.
Since
\tilde{B}_{i}\to B_{i}
is injective,

\nu
induces a map
E\to\tilde{B}_{i}
. As
\tilde{A}
is integral over
E
, the image of
\tilde{A}
in
B_{i}
is integral over
\tilde{B}_{i}
.
Since
\tilde{B}_{i}
is integrally closed in
B_{i}
, we conclude that the map
(h_{i})_{|\tilde{A}}\colon\tilde{A}\to B_{i}
factors via
\tilde{h}_{i}\colon\tilde{A}\to\tilde{B}_{i}
.
If
\tilde{g}_{i}
is ift, so is
\tilde{h}_{i}
by
[
Tem13
, Proposition 2.2.5]

∎

Remark 1.4
.

In the situation of Lemma
1.3
, if
g_{i}
is of finite presentation, so is
h_{i}
by
[
Sta16
,
Tag 00F4
]
.
If both
f
and
g_{i}
are étale, then
h_{i}
is étale by

[
Sta16
,
Tag 02GW
]
.

Lemma 1.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of schemes. Assume that

(1)

f is ift

(2)

X
is qcqs

(3)

S
is quasi-separated

Then there exists
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to S
of finite presentation and
X\to X^{\prime}
integral that factor
f
. If
X\to S
is separated, then
X^{\prime}\to S
is separated.

Proof.

This is a version of
[
Sta16
,
Tag 01ZG
]
and we follow its proof.
By
[
Sta16
,
Tag 01ZA
]
we can write

X=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i\in I}X_{i}
with
I
a directed set,
X_{i}
of finite presentation over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
, and with affine transition maps
X_{i^{\prime}}\to X_{i}
. Then, take an affine open covering
X=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{n}
such that each
V_{j}
maps to an affine open
U_{j}\subseteq S
and the corresponding map of rings is ift,
i.e.
it factors as
\mathcal{O}(U_{j})\to R\to\mathcal{O}(V_{j})
with
\mathcal{O}(U_{j})\to R
of finite type and
R\to\mathcal{O}(V_{j})
integral. By
[
Sta16
,
Tag 01Z4
]
,
and
[
Sta16
,
Tag 01Z6
]
, we can write
V_{j}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i\in I}V_{ij}
for
1\leq j\leq n
, such that
V_{ij}
are of finite presentation over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
, we have

V_{j}=V_{ij}\times_{X_{i}}X
, and
X_{i}=V_{i1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{in}
is an affine open covering for each
i\in I
. For each
j
, choose finitely many
h_{ja}\in R

that generate
R
as a
\mathcal{O}(U_{j})
-algebra and consider their image
k_{ja}
in
\mathcal{O}(V_{j})
.
Choose
i_{0}
such that the
k_{ja}
come from
\mathcal{O}(V_{i_{0}j})
, for all
j
and
a
.
In particular, this implies that the map
V_{j}\to V_{i_{0}j}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})}U_{j}=:W_{j}
is integral. Moreover,
X^{\prime}:=\cup_{j=1}^{n}W_{j}

is an open subscheme of
X_{i_{0},S}=X_{i_{0}}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}}S
, which contains the image of
X\to X_{i_{0},S}
.
Thus
f
factors as
X\to X^{\prime}\to S
with
X\to X^{\prime}
integral.
As
X_{i_{0}}
and
S
are quasi-separated
2
2

2

Note that morphisms
of finite presentation are quasi-separated by definition.
so is
X_{i_{0},S}
.
Recall that if
T
is quasi-separated and
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is an affine open, then the open immersion
T^{\prime}\to T
is quasicompact by
[
Sta16
,
Tag 01K4
and
Tag 01KO
]
.
Thus
W_{j}\to X_{i_{0},S}
is a quasi-compact open immersion and hence so is
X^{\prime}\to X_{i_{0},S}
. It follows that
X^{\prime}\to X_{i_{0},S}
is of finite presentation, and therefore also
X^{\prime}\to S
.

∎

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be an integral morphism and
j\colon Y\to\overline{Y}
a quasi-compact open immersion.
Then there exists a cartesian diagram with
g
an integral morphism

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}.}

Proof.

We may take
\overline{X}
to be the integral closure of
\overline{Y}
in
X
. More precisely,
by assumption the composition
j\circ f
is quasi-compact and quasi separated and hence
(j\circ f)_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a quasi-coherent

\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Y}}
-module. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the subsheaf which over an open
V\subset\overline{Y}
is given by

\mathcal{A}(V)=\{s\in\mathcal{O}_{X}((j\circ f)^{-1}V)\mid s\text{ is integral over }\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Y}}(V)\}.

Then
\mathcal{A}
is a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Y}}
-algebra and

j\circ f
factors as
X\xrightarrow{u}\overline{X}:=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{Y}
with
g
integral.
As
f
is integral we have
g^{-1}(Y)=X
by construction.
∎

The following result will be used in Section
3
.
An analogous result for proper morphisms instead of ift morphisms is
[
KS24
, Lemma 4.14.(2)]
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism between quasi-compact quasi-separated schemes, which is ift, separated, and universally closed,
and let
U\subset Y
be a quasi-compact open such that the base change

X_{U}=X\times_{Y}U\to U
is an isomorphism. Then
X=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i}X_{i}
is a directed limit with
finite
Y
-morphisms
\tau:X_{j}\to X_{i}
(
j\geq i
) as transition maps inducing injections
\tau^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}\hookrightarrow\tau_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{j}}
,
such that each
X_{i}\to Y
is proper and an isomorphism over
U
.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.5
we can factor
f
as
X\xrightarrow{h}X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g}Y
with
h
integral
and
g
separated and of finite type. Let
Z\subset X^{\prime}
denote the scheme-theoretic image of
h
.
As
f
is universally closed
[
Sta16
,
Tag 0AH6
]

yields that
f
factors as
X\xrightarrow{h_{1}}Z\xrightarrow{g_{1}}Y
with
g_{1}
proper and
h_{1}
integral and surjective.
Now consider
\mathcal{A}=h_{1*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
. It is an integral quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
-algebra and can hence be written
as a directed colimit
\mathcal{A}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}\mathcal{A}_{i}
of its finite quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
-subalgebras.
Thus
X=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}X_{i}
is a directed limit with
X_{i}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{A}_{i}
finite over
Z
and hence also finite transition maps

\tau:X_{j}\to X_{i}
which by construction are induced by the inclusions
\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}\hookrightarrow\tau_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{j}}
inside
\mathcal{A}
.
Clearly, the composition
X_{i}\to Z\to Y
is proper. It remains to show that the projection

(X_{i})_{U}\to U
is an isomorphism, for all
i
.

The question is local on
U
and we can therefore assume
U=\operatorname{Spec}R
is affine.
Hence
X_{U}
is affine as well and the projection induces an isomorphism

R\xrightarrow{\simeq}A:=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X_{U}).

By
[
Sta16
,
Tag 01Z6
]
, the scheme
(X_{i})_{U}
is affine for
i
big enough, so by
[
Con07
, Corollary A.2]
,
(X_{i})_{U}
is affine for all
i
, therefore the map
X_{U}\to(X_{i})_{U}
is induced by an integral ring extension
A_{i}\hookrightarrow A
.
Moreover, the section
\sigma_{i}:U\xrightarrow{\simeq}X_{U}\to(X_{i})_{U}
of the projection corresponds to a surjection
of rings
A_{i}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow R
which factors via the isomorphism
A\xrightarrow{\simeq}R
and is therefore injective as well.
This shows that
\sigma_{i}
is an isomorphism inverse to the projection.
∎

2.
Tame topos of pairs

We fix some notations and recall definitions from, e.g.,
[
GR03
, 6.2]
,
[
HS20
, 2]
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valuation field with valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}
and maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{v}
.
Fix an embedding into
(\bar{K},\bar{v})
, where
\bar{K}
is a separable closure of
K
and

\bar{v}
is an extension of
v
to
\bar{K}
. We denote by
(K_{v}^{sh},v^{sh})
the strict henselization
of
(K,v)
(inside
(\bar{K},\bar{v})
).
A finite separable extension
(L,w)/(K,v)
of valuation fields is called
unramified
(resp.
tame
), if
K_{v}^{sh}=L_{w}^{sh}
(resp.

([L_{w}^{sh}:K_{v}^{sh}],p)=1
, where
p
is the exponential characteristic of the residue field of
K
).
The
tame closure

(K^{t},v^{t})
of
(K,v)
is the union of all finite tame Galois extensions of
(K^{sh},v^{sh})
.
The field
K^{t}
is also the fixed field of
\bar{K}
under the tame ramification group

R_{\bar{v}/v}:=\{\sigma\in\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{K}/K)\mid\sigma(\mathcal{O}_{\bar{v}})\subset\mathcal{O}_{\bar{v}}\text{ and }\sigma(x)/x-1\in\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{v}}\textrm{ for all }x\in\bar{K}^{\times}\}.

We record the following well-known lemma for later reference.

Lemma 2.1
.

(1)

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be a finite separable extension of valuation fields.
Let
N/K
be a Galois hull of
L/K
and let
\tilde{w}
be an extension of
w
to
N
.
Then
(L,w)/(K,v)
is tame if and only if
(N,\tilde{w})/(K,v)
is tame.

In particular
(L,w)/(K,v)
is tame if and only if
(L,w)
is a subextension of
(K^{t},v^{t})/(K,v)
.

(2)

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be a tame extension and let
(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})/(K,v)
be any algebraic extension of valuation fields.
Let
L\cdot K^{\prime}
be the composition field in an algebraic closure of
K
and let
w^{\prime}
be a valuation extending
v^{\prime}
.
Then
(L\cdot K^{\prime},w^{\prime})/(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
is tame.

Proof.

(1)
. Note that
N^{sh}_{\tilde{w}}
is a Galois hull of
L^{sh}_{w}/K^{sh}_{v}
.
Therefore we may assume
K,L,N
are strictly henselian valuation fields of characteristic
p>0
.
Thus if
(N,\tilde{w})/(K,v)
is tame then
[N:K]=[N:L]\cdot[L:K]
is prime to
p
and hence
(L,w)/(K,v)
is tame as well.
Now assume
(L,w)/(K,v)
is tame. Denote by
G_{K}\supset G_{L}\supset G_{N}
the absolute Galois groups with respect to
a fixed separable closure
\bar{K}
of
K
, and by
P
the pro-
p
-Sylow subgroup of
G_{K}
, which is a normal subgroup.
The indices satisfy the following equality (of supernatural numbers)

[G_{K}:G_{L}]\cdot[G_{L}:P\cap G_{L}]=[G_{K}:P]\cdot[P:P\cap G_{L}].

As
P
is a normal subgroup of
G_{K}
, the intersection
P\cap G_{L}
is a normal subgroup of
G_{L}

and we have an inclusion of profinite groups
G_{L}/G_{L}\cap P\hookrightarrow G_{K}/P
.
Hence
[G_{K}:P]
and
[G_{L}:P\cap G_{L}]
are prime to
p
.
By assumption
[G_{K}:G_{L}]=[L:K]
is prime to
p
as well. Thus
[P:P\cap G_{L}]=1
, i.e.,
P=P\cap G_{L}
.
The Galois hull of
L/K
is the composition field (inside
\bar{K}
) of all the
\sigma(L)
,
where
\sigma
runs through all the embeddings
L\hookrightarrow\bar{K}
. Extending these
\sigma
’s to
K
-automorphisms of
\bar{K}
,
we find
G_{\sigma(L)}=\sigma G_{L}\sigma^{-1}
. Hence
G_{N}=\cap_{\sigma}\sigma G_{L}\sigma^{-1}
.
As
P
is a normal subgroup of
G_{K}
it follows that
P
is contained in
G_{N}
as well.
Thus
[G_{K}:P]=[G_{K}:G_{N}]\cdot[G_{N}:P]
is prime to
p
and hence so is
[N:K]=[G_{K}:G_{N}]
.

(2)
follows from
(1)
and the fact that
{K^{\prime}}^{t}=K^{\prime}\cdot K^{t}
,
see
[
GR03
, 6.2.18]
.
∎

Recall that for any morphism of schemes
X\to\tilde{X}
,
\mathrm{Spa}(X,\tilde{X})
is the topological space whose underlying set is the set of triples
(x,v,\varepsilon)
such that
x\in X
is a point,
v
is a valuation on
k(x)
and
\varepsilon\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\to\tilde{X}
is a map compatible with
\operatorname{Spec}(k(x))\to X
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
S\to\widetilde{S}
be a morphism of schemes.

(1)

Let
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
be the category of pairs
(X,\tilde{X})
, where
X\to\tilde{X}
is a map of qcqs
\widetilde{S}
-schemes
such that
X\to\widetilde{S}
factors through
S
.
Morphisms
(X,\tilde{X})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
are pairs of morphisms
f:X\to Y
and
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\to\tilde{Y}
satisfying the obvious compatibility.

(2)

A morphism
(f,\tilde{f}):(X,\tilde{X})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
is a
modification
if
f
is an isomorphism and
\tilde{f}
is proper, and
integral birational
if
f
is an isomorphism and
\tilde{f}
is integral. We say that
(f,\tilde{f})
is a
quasi-modification
if
f
is an isomorphism and
\tilde{f}
is ift, separated and universally closed.

(3)

A morphism
(f,\tilde{f}):(V,\tilde{V})\to(U,\tilde{U})
in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
is
strict étale
if
\tilde{f}
is étale,
V=\tilde{V}\times_{\tilde{U}}U
and
f=\tilde{f}\times_{\tilde{U}}{\operatorname{Id}}
.

(4)

A morphism
(f,\tilde{f}):(V,\tilde{V})\to(U,\tilde{U})
in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
is
tame over
(x,v,\varepsilon_{v})\in\mathrm{Spa}(U,\tilde{U})
if
f
is étale over a neighborhood of
x
and there is
(y,w,\varepsilon_{w})\in\mathrm{Spa}(V,\tilde{V})
such that
f(y)=x
,
w_{|k(x)}=v
, and
w/v
is tamely ramified and the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f)_{|y,w\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{v}}

A morphism
(f,\tilde{f}):(V,\tilde{V})\to(U,\tilde{U})
is a
tame covering
if it is tame over all points of
\mathrm{Spa}(U,\tilde{U})
.

Remark 2.3
.

(1)

By Raynaud–Gruson
[
Sta16
,
Tag 081T
]
, every modification
(f,\tilde{f}):(V,\tilde{V})\to(U,\tilde{U})
is dominated by an
admissible blow-up
, i.e. a blow-up
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}:{\mathbf{Bl}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{U})\to\tilde{U}
in a quasi-coherent ideal
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}
of finite type such that the support of
\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}/\mathcal{A}
is contained in
\tilde{U}\setminus U
.

(2)

Note that by the valuative criterion, if
\tilde{f}\colon\tilde{U}^{\prime}\to\tilde{U}
is separated and universally closed and
U\to\tilde{U}^{\prime}
is any map, we have a bijection
\mathrm{Spa}(U,\tilde{U})\cong\mathrm{Spa}(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})
.

(3)

By Theorem
1.7
, every quasi-modification
(f,\tilde{f})\colon(X,\tilde{X})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
is a cofiltered limit of modifications
(f_{i},\tilde{f}_{i})\colon(X_{i},\tilde{X}_{i})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
such that for every
i
the maps
(X,\tilde{X})\to(X_{i},\tilde{X}_{i})
are integral birational.

The two sites below are very much inspired by the definition of the étale and tame site of a Huber pair (see
[
Hüb21
]
and
[
HS20
]
).

Definition 2.4
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
be a quasi-compact open immersion of qcqs schemes.
Let
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(X,\tilde{X})}

consisting of objects
(f,\tilde{f})\colon(U,\tilde{U})\to(X,\tilde{X})

with
f
étale and
\tilde{f}
ift, and
U\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}
a quasi-compact open immersion. This category has fiber products given by

(V_{1},\tilde{V_{1}})\times_{(U,\tilde{U})}(V_{2},\tilde{V_{2}})=(V_{1}\times_{U}V_{2},\tilde{V_{1}}\times_{\tilde{U}}\tilde{V_{2}}),

and terminal object
(X,\tilde{X})
, so for
I
an indexing diagram and
\{(U_{i},\tilde{U}_{i})\}_{i\in I}\subseteq(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
, the limit (if it exists) is computed as
(\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i}U_{i},\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}\tilde{U}_{i})
.
On this category, we will consider the following three topologies:

(1)

The
strict étale
topology which is generated by strict étale coverings

(2)

The
v-étale
topology which is generated by strict étale coverings and quasi-modifications.

(3)

The
tame
topology which is generated by tame coverings, where a family
\{(f_{i},\tilde{f}_{i}):(V_{i},\tilde{V}_{i})\to(U,\tilde{U})\}_{i}
of maps in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
is a tame covering if for every
(x,v,\varepsilon_{v})\in\mathrm{Spa}(U,\tilde{U})
,
there is
i\in I
such that
(V_{i},\tilde{V}_{i})\to(U,\tilde{U})
is tame over
(x,v,\varepsilon_{v})
.

We let
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}}
,
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
and
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
denote the strict étale, the v-étale and the tame site on
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
, respectively.
We have morphisms of sites:

(2.4.1)

(X,\tilde{X})_{t}\overset{\nu}{\longrightarrow}(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}\overset{\mu}{\longrightarrow}(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}}

corresponding to the inclusion functors.

Remark 2.5
.

(1)

The tame topology is finitary, i.e., any covering can be refined by a covering of the form

g:(V,\tilde{V})\to(U,\tilde{U})
for
(V,\tilde{V})
and
(U,\tilde{U})
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
.
This follows from the fact that
\mathrm{Spa}(X,\tilde{X})

is a quasi-compact topological space by
[
HS20
, Lemma 4.3]
with the topology defined there.

(2)

Let
(U,\tilde{U})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
and let
\overline{U}
be the closure of
U
in
\tilde{U}
. Then
(U,\overline{U})\to(U,\tilde{U})
is a quasi-modification, hence it is a v-étale covering and a tame covering.

(3)

Note that for any
(U,\tilde{U})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, there exists a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{U}}

of finite type
such that the support of
\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{U}}/\mathcal{I}
is equal to
\tilde{U}\setminus U
.
This uses that
U\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}
is a quasi-compact open immersion
and that
\tilde{U}
is qcqs.
In particular, blowing up
\tilde{U}
in such an ideal we obtain
a modification
(U,\bar{U})\to(U,\tilde{U})
such that the complement
\bar{U}\setminus U
is the support of an effective Cartier divisor.

Example 2.6
.

Consider the presheaf
\mathcal{O}^{t}
on
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
given by

\mathcal{O}^{t}(U,\tilde{U})=\mathcal{O}(\tilde{U}^{\rm int})
for
(U,\tilde{U})\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
,
where
\tilde{U}^{\rm int}
denotes the integral closure of
\tilde{U}
in
U
.
We will see in Lemma
8.5
that this defines a tame sheaf.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})\to(X,\tilde{X})
be a quasi-modification.
Then, for any sheaf
F\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})
and
(U,\tilde{U})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
, we have an isomorphism

F(U,\tilde{U})\cong F(U,\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{X}^{\prime}).

In particular, the restriction functors along the inclusion
(X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{\tau}\to(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}

{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})\;\text{ and }\;{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X})_{t})\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{t})

are equivalences of topoi so that for a presheaf
F
of abelian groups, we have equivalences

R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}},a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}F)\cong R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}},a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}F)

and

R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X})_{t},a_{t}F)\cong R\Gamma((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{t},a_{t}F),

where
a_{t}
and
a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
denote the respective sheafification functor.

Proof.

We prove it for
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
. The proof for
t
is the same.
Let
F
be a sheaf of sets on
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
. For
(U,\tilde{U})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}=\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{X}^{\prime}
,
the map induced by projection
(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})\to(U,\tilde{U})
and the map induced by the diagonal

\delta:(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})\to(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime}\times_{\tilde{U}}\tilde{U}^{\prime})
are modifications and thus are coverings in
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
. Hence,

\delta^{*}:F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime}\times_{\tilde{U}}\tilde{U}^{\prime})\to F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})

is injective and we find

\displaystyle F(U,\tilde{U})

\displaystyle\cong{\rm eq}\left(F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})\overset{pr_{1}^{*}}{\underset{pr_{2}^{*}}{\rightrightarrows}}F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime}\times_{\tilde{U}}\tilde{U}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\cong{\rm eq}\left(F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})\overset{id}{\underset{id}{\rightrightarrows}}F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})\right)=F(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime}).

Hence, the functor

{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}),\quad G\mapsto\left((U,\tilde{U})\mapsto G(U,\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{X}^{\prime})\right)

gives a quasi-inverse of the restiction functor
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}}((X,\tilde{X}^{\prime})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})
.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

Let
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},\tau}
be the subcategory of
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
whose objects are pairs
(U,\tilde{U})
, with
U
and
\tilde{U}
affine,
equipped with the
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
and tame topologies by restriction.

Let
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}
be the full subcategory of
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},\tau}
whose objects are pairs
(U,\tilde{U})

with
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{U})\to\mathcal{O}(U)
injective and integrally closed, equipped with v-étale and tame topologies by restriction.

Lemma 2.9
.

The inclusion functors
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}\to(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
and

(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},t}\to(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
induce equivalences on the associated topoi.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check the properties (1)-(5) of
[
Sta16
,
Tag 03A0
]
.
∎

3.
Computation of the v-étale topology

In this section we study some properties of the v-étale topology.

Lemma 3.1
.

For every composition
\mathcal{V}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}}\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{2}}\mathcal{Y}
in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(S,\widetilde{S})}
with
\varphi_{2}
strict étale and
\varphi_{1}
a quasi-modification, there is a quasi-modification
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that
\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
factors through a quasi-modification
\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

First, we show that the result holds for
\varphi_{1}
a modification. This is classical (see
[
MVW06
, Proposition 12.27]
):
let
\mathcal{U}=(U,\tilde{U})
,
\mathcal{V}=(V,\tilde{V})
, and
\mathcal{Y}=(Y,\tilde{Y})
. As observed in
Remark
2.5
(2)
, we can assume that
Y
dense in
\tilde{Y}
.
By Raynaud–Gruson
[
Sta16
,
Tag 081R
]
, there exists a

Y
-admissible blow-up
\tilde{T}\to\tilde{Y}
such that the strict transform
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
of
\tilde{V}
over
\tilde{T}
is flat of finite presentation over
\tilde{T}
.
Note that the map
\tilde{V}^{\prime}\to\tilde{T}
factors as

\tilde{V}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{Y}}\tilde{T}\xrightarrow{\beta}\tilde{T}
, where
\beta
is étale and
\alpha
is proper inducing an isomorphism over the dense open
U=\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{Y}}\tilde{T}\times_{\tilde{Y}}Y
. Moreover,
\alpha
is flat by

[
KS24
, Lemma 4.15]
and thus an isomorphism by
[
KS24
, Lemma 4.16]
.
Hence, we get a morphism
\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{Y}}\tilde{T}\to\tilde{V}
, which is proper and an isomorphism over
U
.

Let now
\varphi_{1}
be a quasi-modification. By Remark
2.3
(3), there exists
\mathcal{V}_{0}=(U,\tilde{V}_{0})
such that
\varphi_{1}
factors as a composition of an integral birational morphism
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}_{0}
and a modification
\mathcal{V}_{0}\to\mathcal{U}
.
By the previous paragraph, there exists a modification
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(Y,\tilde{T}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{Y}
such that for
\tilde{U}^{\prime}:=\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{Y}}\tilde{T}^{\prime}
, the map
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=(U,\tilde{U}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{U}
factors through
\mathcal{V}_{0}
, hence we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{V}_{0}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}

with
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
integral birational,
\varphi_{2}^{\prime}
strict étale and
q_{0}
and
\psi_{0}
modifications.
Let
\tilde{T}
be the integral closure of
\tilde{T}^{\prime}
in
Y
and put

\tilde{W}:=\tilde{U}^{\prime}\times_{\tilde{T}^{\prime}}\tilde{T}\cong\tilde{U}\times_{\tilde{Y}}\tilde{T}
.
Then,

\tilde{W}
is the integral closure of
\tilde{U}^{\prime}
in
U
by
[
Sta16
,
Tag 03GE
]

since
\tilde{U}^{\prime}\to\tilde{T}^{\prime}
is étale and
U=\tilde{U}^{\prime}\times_{\tilde{T}^{\prime}}Y
. As
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
is integral birational, the
\tilde{U}^{\prime}
-scheme
\tilde{W}
is isomorphic to
the integral closure of
\tilde{V}\times_{\tilde{V}_{0}}\tilde{U}^{\prime}
in
U
.
Hence
\tilde{W}\to\tilde{U}^{\prime}
factors via
\tilde{W}=(\tilde{V}\times_{\tilde{V}_{0}}\tilde{U}^{\prime})^{\rm int}\to\tilde{V}\times_{\tilde{V}_{0}}\tilde{U}^{\prime}
.
Therefore we can take
\mathcal{T}=(Y,\tilde{T})
and this completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

For
F\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}}})
and
\mathcal{U}\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
, we have

(3.2.1)

a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}(F)(\mathcal{U})=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}}F(\tilde{V})=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}}F(\tilde{W})

where the first colimit runs along all quasi-modifications
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{V}=(V,\tilde{V})
and
\tilde{V}
integrally closed in
V
,
and the second colimit runs along all admissible blow-ups
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha F
be the presheaf defined by

\mathcal{U}\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}\mapsto\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U}}F(\tilde{U}^{\prime}),

where the colimit runs through all quasi-modifications. By taking the integral closure,
every quasi-modification is dominated by a quasi-modification
\mathcal{V}=(V,\tilde{V})\to\mathcal{U}
with
\tilde{V}
integrally closed in
V
,
and by Theorem
1.7
, every quasi-modification is a cofiltered limit of modifications, and by Raynaud–Gruson
[
Sta16
,
Tag 081T
]
, any modification is dominated by an admissible blow-up. Therefore the two colimits in (
3.2.1
) are isomorphic to
\alpha F(\mathcal{U})
.
First, we claim that
\alpha F\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})
. Indeed, by construction
\alpha F
sends quasi-modifications to isomorphisms, so it has descent for those coverings. It remains to prove that
\alpha F
has descent for every strict étale covering
\{\mathcal{U}_{i}\to\mathcal{U}\}_{i\in I}
. By a standard reduction, we may assume
I=\{1,2\}
.
Using
F\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}})
, for any quasi-modification
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
, we have

F(\mathcal{V})=F(\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}_{1})\times_{F(\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}_{12})}F(\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}_{2}),

where
\mathcal{U}_{12}=\mathcal{U}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
.
Taking the colimit over
\mathcal{V}
and using Lemma
3.1
and the fact that filtered colimits commute with fiber products, we get

\alpha F(\mathcal{U})=\alpha F(\mathcal{U}_{1})\times_{\alpha F(\mathcal{U}_{12})}\alpha F(\mathcal{U}_{2}).

Thus, we get a functor

\alpha:\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}})\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})
.
It suffices to show that it is a left adjoint of the inclusion
i:\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}})\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}})
.
By construction we have a natural transformation
{\rm id}\to i\alpha
and by Lemma
2.7
also
a natural isomorphism
\alpha i\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\rm id}
.
The statement thus follows from
[
Mac71
, IV, §1, Theorem 2(v)]
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
I
be a sheaf of abelian groups on
(X,\tilde{X})_{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}
.
If
I
is flabby, that is

H^{i}(\mathcal{V}_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}},F)=0
, for any
i>0
and
\mathcal{V}\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
, then
a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I
is flabby as a
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
sheaf.

Proof.

By
[
Mil80
, III, Proposition 2.12]
,
I
is flabby as a
{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}
sheaf if and only if
for any
\mathcal{U}\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
and a strict étale covering
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U}
,
the Čech complex

0\to I(\mathcal{U})\to I(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\to I(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\to\cdots

is exact. This implies that for any modification
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
, the Čech complex

0\to I(\mathcal{V})\to I(\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\to I(\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\to\cdots

is also exact. Noting that filtered colimits are exact, Lemma
3.2
together with Lemma
3.1

implies

0\to a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I(\mathcal{U})\to a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\to a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\to\cdots

is exact. Noting
a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I(\mathcal{U})\simeq a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I(\mathcal{V})
for any modification
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
,
this implies that
a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I
is flabby on
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
again by
[
Mil80
, III, Proposition 2.12]
.
∎

Definition 3.4
.

For
\mathcal{U}=(U,\tilde{U})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
, let
\lambda^{\mathcal{U}}\colon(U,\tilde{U})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}\to\tilde{U}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
be the morphism of sites defined by the functor

\tilde{U}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\to(U,\tilde{U})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}},\quad\tilde{V}\mapsto(\tilde{V}\times_{\tilde{U}}U,\tilde{V}).

It is clear by construction

H^{q}((U,\tilde{U})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}},F)=H^{q}(\tilde{U}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\lambda^{\mathcal{U}}_{*}F).

We can now compare the
{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}
cohomology with the étale cohomologies of the sheaves
\lambda_{*}^{\mathcal{U}}F
. This result should be compared with
[
Hübb
, Corollary 5.7]
, as well as
[
BPØ22
, Theorem 5.1.2]
and
[
KMSY21
, Theorem 2]
, where analogous results were obtained.

Lemma 3.5
.

For a sheaf of abelian groups
F
on
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
and
\mathcal{U}=(U,\tilde{U})
, there is a natural isomorphism

H^{i}(\mathcal{U}_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}},F)=\varinjlim_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}}H^{i}(\tilde{V}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\lambda^{\mathcal{V}}_{*}F)=\varinjlim_{\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}}H^{i}(\tilde{W}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\lambda^{\mathcal{W}}_{*}F),

where the first colimit runs along all quasi-modifications
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{V}=(V,\tilde{V})
and
\tilde{V}

integrally closed in
V
, and the second colimit runs along all admissible blow-ups
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

This is very similar to
[
BPØ22
, Theorem 5.1.2]
.
Let
F_{|(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}}}\to I^{\bullet}
be a flabby resolution of the restriction of
F
to
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}}
.
By Lemma
3.3
, this gives a flabby resolution
F\to a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I^{\bullet}
on
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.2

H^{i}(\mathcal{U}_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}},F)=\pi_{-i}(a_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}I^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}))=\pi_{-i}(\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}}I^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}))=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}}H^{i}(\mathcal{V}_{{\operatorname{s\acute{e}t}}},F)=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}}H^{i}(\tilde{V}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\lambda^{\mathcal{V}}_{*}F).

∎

4.
Acyclic objects

In this section, we show the existence of acyclic pro-covers, Lemma
4.4
, which will be used in
the comparison of the tame cohomology with Čech cohomology (see Lemma
6.4
).

Definition 4.1
.

We let
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}_{(X,\tilde{X})}
consisting of affine pairs
(U,\tilde{U})
such that there exists a cofiltered system
\{(U_{i},\tilde{U}_{i})\}_{i\in I}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},\tau}
such that
U=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}U_{i}
and
\tilde{U}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}\tilde{U}_{i}
as schemes. Notice that in this case the map
U\to X
is no longer in general étale (but rather, pro-étale), the
map
U\to\tilde{U}
is no longer in general a quasi-compact open immersion and the map
\tilde{U}\to\tilde{X}
is no longer ift.

We also consider the full subcategory
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}}
of
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
whose objects are limits of
(U_{i},\tilde{U}_{i})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}
(cf. Definition
2.8
).

Remark 4.2
.

For
\mathcal{Y}=(\operatorname{Spec}(A),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}))\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}}
, we observe that:

(1)

\tilde{A}\to A
is injective and integrally closed, as filtered colimits are exact and the integral closure commutes with filtered colimits.

(2)

If
A=A_{1}\times A_{2}
is a product of rings and
\tilde{A}_{i}
are the integral closures of
\tilde{A}
in
A_{i}
for
i=1,2
,
we have
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{Y}_{2}
with
\mathcal{Y}_{i}=(\operatorname{Spec}(A_{i}),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}_{i}))
.

Definition 4.3
.

We say that
(T,\tilde{T})\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
is
v-étale (resp. tamely) acyclic

if for every v-étale (resp. tame) covering
(V,\tilde{V})\to(U,\tilde{U})
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
and a map
\psi:(T,\tilde{T})\to(U,\tilde{U})
in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
, there exists a map
(T,\tilde{T})\to(V,\tilde{V})
which lifts
\psi
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For every
\mathcal{Y}\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
, there is
\mathcal{W}\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
v-étale (resp. tamely) acyclic such that
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{Y}
is a cofiltered limit of v-étale (resp. tame) coverings

\mathcal{W}_{i}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},\tau}
with
\mathcal{W}_{i}\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma only for the tame topology. The proof for the v-étale topology is the same.
We can suppose
\mathcal{Y}:=(\operatorname{Spec}(A),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}))\in(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
.
We use the same strategy of
[
BS15
, Lemma 2.2.7]
(see
[
HS20
, Proposition 7.12]
). Let
I
be the set of isomorphism classes of coverings
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
. For each
i\in I
, pick a representative
(\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i}),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{B}_{i}))\to\mathcal{Y}
and set

(4.4.1)

A_{1}:=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{J\subset I\;\mathrm{finite}}\bigotimes_{j\in J}B_{j}\qquad\tilde{A}_{1}:=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{J\subset I\;\mathrm{finite}}\bigotimes_{j\in J}\tilde{B}_{j},

where the tensor products are over
A
and
\tilde{A}
, respectively.
By construction, we can write

\mathcal{Y}_{1}:=(\operatorname{Spec}(A_{1}),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}_{1}))=\varprojlim_{\lambda_{1}\in\Lambda_{1}}\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}\;\text{ with }\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}=(\operatorname{Spec}(A_{\lambda_{1}}),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}_{\lambda_{1}}))

as a cofiltered limit of coverings
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
such that for every covering
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, the map
\mathcal{Y}_{1}\to\mathcal{Y}
factors through
\mathcal{U}
.
For each
\lambda_{1}\in\Lambda_{1}
, let
I_{\lambda_{1}}
be the set of isomorphism classes of coverings
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
and apply the same construction as (
4.4.1
) to
(\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}},I_{\lambda_{1}})
instead of
(\mathcal{Y},I)
to get

(A_{2,\lambda_{1}},\tilde{A}_{2,\lambda_{1}})
instead of
(A_{1},\tilde{A}_{1})
and put
\mathcal{Y}_{2,\lambda_{1}}=(\operatorname{Spec}(A_{2,\lambda_{1}}),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}_{2,\lambda_{1}}))
.
Then, for every covering
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}
, the map
\mathcal{Y}_{2,\lambda_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}
factors through
\mathcal{U}
. Put

(4.4.2)

A_{2}:=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{J\subset\Lambda_{1}\;\mathrm{finite}}\bigotimes_{\lambda_{1}\in J}(A_{2,\lambda_{1}}\otimes_{A_{\lambda_{1}}}A_{1}),\qquad\tilde{A}_{2}:=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{J\subset\Lambda_{1}\;\mathrm{finite}}\bigotimes_{\lambda_{1}\in J}(\tilde{A}_{2,\lambda_{1}}\otimes_{\tilde{A}_{\lambda_{1}}}\tilde{A}_{1}),

where the tensor products are over
A_{1}
and
\tilde{A}_{1}
, respectively.
Noting
\mathcal{Y}_{2,\lambda_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{1}\to\mathcal{Y}
are cofiltered limits of coverings,
we can write

\mathcal{Y}_{2}:=(\operatorname{Spec}(A_{2}),\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{A}_{2}))=\varprojlim_{\lambda_{2}\in\Lambda_{2}}\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}}

as a cofiltered limit
of coverings
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
such that for every
\lambda_{1}\in\Lambda_{1}
and covering
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, the map

\mathcal{Y}_{2}\to\mathcal{Y}_{1}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}}
factors through
\mathcal{U}
.
Iterating the construction, we get a sequence in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}

\cdots\to\mathcal{Y}_{3}\to\mathcal{Y}_{2}\to\mathcal{Y}_{1}\to\mathcal{Y}\,\text{ with }\;\mathcal{Y}_{n}=\varprojlim_{\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}

such that for every
\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda_{n}
,

\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}\to\mathcal{Y}
is a covering in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
and for every covering
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, the map

\mathcal{Y}_{n+1}\to\mathcal{Y}_{n}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}
factors through
\mathcal{U}
.
Set
\mathcal{W}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{Y}_{n}\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
. By the construction,
\mathcal{W}
is a cofiltered limit of tame coverings of
\mathcal{Y}
.
It remains to show that
\mathcal{W}
is tamely acyclic. Let
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
be a covering in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\rm{affine},t}
and

\varphi:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}
be a map in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
.
By Lemma
1.3
(with
(f,\tilde{f})
induced by
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{X}
),
\varphi
factors through
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}
for some
\lambda_{n}
.
Since
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}
is a covering in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, the map
\mathcal{Y}_{n+1}\to\mathcal{Y}_{n}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}
factors through
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{n}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{V}
so that
\varphi
lifts to a map
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{V}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Fiber functors

In this section, we characterize fiber functors of the topoi of the sheaves of sets on
(X,\tilde{X})_{{\operatorname{v\acute{e}t}}}
and
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
(see Proposition
5.2
). First, we recall the following.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a site admitting finite limits.
Recall that a
fiber functor
of a topos
\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}(C,\gamma)
of sheaves of sets, is a functor
\varphi:\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}(C,\gamma)\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Set}}}
which preserves colimits and finite limits. Let
\mathrm{Fib}(\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}(C,\gamma))
denote the category of fiber functors of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}(C,\gamma)
.

In what follows, let
\gamma
denote either the v-étale topology or the tame topology on
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
.
By Lemma
2.9
, there is an equivalence of categories of fiber functors

\mathrm{Fib}(\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},\gamma}))\simeq\mathrm{Fib}(\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{\gamma})).

Hence, by
[
Joh77
, Pro.7.13]
and Remark
2.5
(1), there is a bijection between fiber functors of

\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{\gamma})
and cofiltered pro-objects

(5.1.1)

\mathcal{P}_{\bullet}=\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda}{``\varprojlim"}\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}\;\text{ with }\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}=(P,\tilde{P})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},\gamma}

indexed by
a cofiltered category
\Lambda
, which satisfies the
\gamma
-locality condition: For every
\gamma
-covering

\mathcal{V}\overset{u}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{U}
, the morphism of sets

\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\operatorname{Hom}_{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}(\mathcal{P}_{\lambda},\mathcal{V})\to\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\operatorname{Hom}_{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}(\mathcal{P}_{\lambda},\mathcal{U})

is surjective. By Lemma
1.3
, the latter condition is equivalent to that

\mathcal{T}=\varprojlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathcal{P}\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
is
\gamma
-acyclic in the sense of Definition
4.3
and the corresponding fiber functor is given by

(5.1.2)

\varphi_{\mathcal{T}}:\operatorname{\mathbf{Shv}}((X,\tilde{X})_{\gamma})\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Set}}}\;;\;F\to F(\mathcal{T}):=\varinjlim_{\lambda}F(\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}).

Proposition 5.2
.

A pair
\mathcal{T}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i\in I}\mathcal{T}_{i}\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
is v-étale local (resp. tame local) if and only if
\mathcal{T}
is a coproduct of objects of the form
(\operatorname{Spec}(S),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}))
such that

\widetilde{S}
is strictly henselian local and
S
is henselian local
and that
\widetilde{S}=S\times_{k}\mathcal{O}_{v}
, where
k
is the residue field of
S
equipped with a valuation
v
such that
(k,v)
is strictly henselian (resp.
(k,v)
is tamely closed), and
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq k
is the valuation ring.
Moreover, in both cases we have
(T,\tilde{T})\in\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}}
.

Remark 5.3
.

With the terminology from
[
HS20
, Definition 10.8]

the pair
(S,\widetilde{S})
appearing in Proposition
5.2
is a strictly henselian (resp. tamely henselian) local Huber pair.

The analogous result for local Huber pairs is
[
HS20
, Proposition 10.15]
. Our context is different in the following sense: on a discretely ringed adic space
\mathrm{Spa}(X,\tilde{X})
, going “local” means looking at neighborhoods of a point
(x,v,\varepsilon)
for a
fixed
valuation rign
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq k(x)
, while in the case of the site
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
, then one considers neighborhoods of a point
x\in X
and their compactifications mapping to to
\tilde{X}
, which means that we need to consider
all
the possible valuation rings
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq k(x)
with a map
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\to\tilde{X}
. This was the same philosophy used in the construction of local modulus pairs of
[
KM
, Corollary 3.5]
, therefore our proof will follow their arguments. For this reason, in order to keep the proof self-contained and more readable, we will write it in details.

We need the following preliminary result:

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(Y,\tilde{Y})=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i\in I}(Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
.
Let
(f,\tilde{f})\colon(U,\tilde{U})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
with
f
an étale covering. Then there exists a cofiltered category
J
and a system of maps

(f_{ij},\tilde{f}_{ij})\colon(U_{ij},\tilde{U}_{ij})\to(Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
indexed over
I\times J
such that for all
(i,j)
,
(U_{ij},\tilde{U}_{ij})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},\tau}
and
f_{ij}
is an étale covering, and
\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{(i,j)\in I\times J}(U_{ij},\tilde{U}_{ij})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
refines
(f,\tilde{f})
.
Moreover, if
(f,\tilde{f})
is a tame covering in the sense of Definition
2.2
, then we find such system that
(f_{ij},\tilde{f}_{ij})
are tame coverings for all
i,j
.

Proof.

By definition,
(U,\tilde{U})
is a limit of
(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},t}
with
j\in J
a cofiltered set,
Let
(U^{\prime},\tilde{U}^{\prime})=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j}^{\rm int})
. Then
(U^{\prime},\tilde{U}^{\prime})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})
is a covering that refines
(U,\tilde{U})
, so we can suppose that
(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},t}
.
Fix
i\in I
. We need to show that

(U,\tilde{U})\to(Y,\tilde{Y})\to(X,\tilde{X})
factors through
(U_{j_{i}},\tilde{U}_{j_{i}})
,
for some
j_{i}\in J
.
To this end, we may assume that
X
and
\tilde{X}
are affine, and Lemma
1.3
gives such factorization.
In particular, we find a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{j_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}_{j_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}_{i},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{ij_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}_{ij_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

in which
f_{ij_{i}}
is étale by Remark
1.4
and
\tilde{f}_{ij_{i}}
are ift, hence it gives a map
(f_{ij_{i}},\tilde{f}_{ij_{i}})
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{t}
.
Moreover, since all the indexing sets are cofiltered, for every
i\in I
,
and every
j\in J
as before, there exists
(U_{j^{\prime}},\tilde{U}_{j^{\prime}})
that maps to both
(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j})
and
(U_{j_{i}},\tilde{U}_{j_{i}})
, hence we find a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{j^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}_{i},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{ij^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}_{ij^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

with
f_{ij^{\prime}}
étale (since
U_{j^{\prime}}\to U_{j_{i}}
and
f_{ij_{i}}
are étale) and
\tilde{f}_{ij^{\prime}}
ift (since
\tilde{U}_{j^{\prime}}\to\tilde{U}_{j_{i}}
are ift by
[
Tem13
, Proposition 2.2.5]

and
f_{ij_{i}}
are étale), then up to replacing
J
by a cofinal set we can suppose that for every
(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j})
there exist
i\in I
,
and a map
(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j})\to(Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
as in the statement with
(U_{j},\tilde{U}_{j})\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},t}
.

Now, assume that
(f,\tilde{f})
is a tame covering and prove that
(f_{ij},\tilde{f}_{ij})
as constructed above
is a tame covering for a sufficiently large
i,j
.
We proceed as the proof of
[
HS20
, Theorem 4.6]
.
Let
Z_{i}\subseteq\mathrm{Spa}(Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
be the set of triples
(y_{i},w_{i},\varepsilon_{w_{i}})
such that there is no
(x_{i},v_{i},\varepsilon_{v_{i}})
in
\mathrm{Spa}(U_{ij},\tilde{U}_{ij})
tame over
(y_{i},w_{i},\varepsilon_{w_{i}})
.
Since
(f,\tilde{f})
is tame, we have
\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{i}Z_{i}=\varnothing
.
By
[
Hüb21
, Cor.4.4]
,
Z_{i}
is closed, which implies that it is compact in the constructible topology by
[
Sta16
,
Tag 0901
]
since
\mathrm{Spa}(Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
is spectral.
Since the inverse limit of nonempty compact spaces is nonempty, we must have
Z_{i}=\emptyset
for a sufficiently large
i
, which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.2
:

First of all, we observe that
T\to\tilde{T}
is dense by Remark
2.5
(2)
, hence
T
and
\tilde{T}
have the same number of connected components.
Let
\tilde{T}=\coprod_{i\in I}\tilde{T}_{i}
and
T=\coprod_{i\in I}T_{i}
be the decomposition into the connected components. We claim that
\mathcal{T}=(T,\tilde{T})
is acyclic if and only if
\mathcal{T}_{i}=(T_{i},\tilde{T}_{i})
are acyclic for all
i
.
Indeed, assume that
\mathcal{T}
is acyclic and take coverings
\mathcal{V}_{i}\to\mathcal{U}_{i}
in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
and maps
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{U}_{i}
in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
for
i\in I
.
Fixing
i\in I
, it gives rise to a covering

\mathcal{V}:=\sqcup_{j\neq i}\mathcal{U}_{j}\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{i}\to\mathcal{U}=\sqcup_{j\in I}\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
a map
\varphi:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
in an obvious way. By the assumption,
\varphi
factors through
\mathcal{V}
and the image of the map
\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{V}
lands in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
since
T_{i}
and
\tilde{T}_{i}
are connected. Thus,
\varphi_{i}
factors through
\mathcal{V}_{i}
showing that
\mathcal{T}_{i}
is acyclic.
On the other hand, assume that
\mathcal{T}_{i}
are acyclic for all
i\in I
.
Let
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
be a covering in
(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}
and
\varphi:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
be a map in
\widetilde{(X,\tilde{X})_{\tau}}
.
For each
i\in I
, the map
\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
factors through a map
\psi_{i}:\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{V}
since
\mathcal{T}_{i}
are acyclic.
Then,
\psi=\sqcup_{i\in I}\psi_{i}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{V}
gives a lift of
\varphi
showing that
\mathcal{T}
is acyclic. For the rest of the proof, we assume that
(T,\tilde{T})
is connected.

\Rightarrow
: Assume
\mathcal{T}
is v-étale local (resp. tame local).
Recall that every v-étale covering is also a tame covering.
Let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\tilde{T})=(\operatorname{Spec}(S),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}))
and write

\mathcal{T}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{\alpha\in A}\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\;\text{ with }\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}))\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm affine},t}

so
S=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{S}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\alpha}\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}
.
Let
(\widetilde{S})^{\rm int}
(resp.
(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}
) be the integral closure of
\widetilde{S}
in
S
(resp.
\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}
in
S_{\alpha}
).
We have
(\widetilde{S})^{\rm int}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varinjlim}_{\alpha}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}
so we have

(\operatorname{Spec}(S),\operatorname{Spec}((\widetilde{S})^{\rm int}))=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{\alpha\in A}(\mathcal{T}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}

with

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}^{\rm int}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}),\operatorname{Spec}((\widetilde{S}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}))\in(X,\tilde{X})_{{\rm int},t}
.
Since
(\mathcal{T}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}\to\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
is a quasi-modification and
\mathcal{T}
is acyclic, the projection

\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
factors through
(\mathcal{T}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}
, i.e.,

\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\to\widetilde{S}
factors through
(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}
. This implies
\widetilde{S}=(\widetilde{S})^{\rm int}
and
\mathcal{T}=\operatornamewithlimits{\varprojlim}_{\alpha\in A}(\mathcal{T}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}
so we may assume
\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}=(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha})^{\rm int}
for all
\alpha\in A
.

Noting that the restriction of the v-étale topology on
\tilde{X}
is finer than the étale topology,
\widetilde{S}
must be strictly henselian.

Next, we show that
S
is a local ring (see
[
KM
, Proposition 3.3, “
\Rightarrow 2
”]
)
Let
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
be closed points in
T
.
Take open covers
\{\operatorname{Spec}(S[1/g_{\nu,j}])\}_{j\in J_{\nu}}
of
T-\{x_{\nu}\}
,
\nu=1,2
.
As
S
is a localization of
\widetilde{S}
we may assume

(5.4.1)

g_{\nu,j}\in\widetilde{S}\setminus\widetilde{S}\cap S^{\times},\quad\text{for all }\nu,j.

If
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, then the ideal
(g_{1,i},g_{2,j})_{i\in J_{1},j\in J_{2}}
of
\widetilde{S}
maps to the unit ideal in
S
.
As
T
is quasi-compact, there exist finite subsets
K_{\nu}\subset J_{\nu}
such that

\bigsqcup_{i\in K_{1}}\operatorname{Spec}(S[1/g_{1,i}])\sqcup\bigsqcup_{j\in K_{2}}\operatorname{Spec}(S[1/g_{2,j}])\to T

is an open covering. In particular the finitely generated ideal

\mathfrak{I}:=(g_{1,i},g_{2,j})_{i\in K_{1},j\in K_{2}}
of
\widetilde{S}
maps to the unit ideal in
S
.
There exists
\alpha
such that
g_{1,i},g_{2,j}
come from
g_{\alpha,1,i},g_{\alpha,2,j}\in\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}
and
the ideal
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}:=(g_{\alpha,1,i},g_{\alpha,2,j})_{i\in K_{1},j\in K_{2}}
of
\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}
maps to the unit ideal in
S_{\alpha}
.
Hence, we get the v-étale covering:

\bigsqcup_{i\in K_{1}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,1,i}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{j\in K_{2}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,2,j}\to\mathcal{T}_{\alpha},

where

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,\nu,i}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}[1/g_{\alpha,\nu,i}]),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}[\tfrac{\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}}{g_{\alpha,\nu,i}}])),\quad\text{for }\nu=1,2\text{ and }i\in K_{\nu}.

Since
\mathcal{T}
is acyclic, the projection
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
factors through

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,\nu,i}
for
\nu=1
or
2
and some
i\in K_{\nu}
, which implies
that
g_{1,i}
or
g_{2,i}
is a unit in
S
, contradicting (
5.4.1
).
Therefore
x_{1}=x_{2}
and
S
is local.

Let
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\widetilde{S}
be the prime ideal such that
\mathfrak{p}S
is the maximal ideal of
S
.
We show that
\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}
is a valuation ring (see
[
KM
, Proposition 3.3, “
\Rightarrow 3
”]
). Let
a,b\in\widetilde{S}\setminus\mathfrak{p}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}S
is maximal,
a
and
b
are invertible in
S
. There exists
\alpha\in A
such that
a,b
come from

a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}
.
Then,
we have the v-étale covering
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,a}\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,b}\to\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
with

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,a}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\Bigl[\frac{b_{\alpha}}{a_{\alpha}}\Bigr])),\quad\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,b}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\Bigl[\frac{a_{\alpha}}{b_{\alpha}}\Bigr])).

Since
\mathcal{T}
is acyclic and connected, the projection
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
factors through

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,a}
or
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,b}
, so
\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\to\widetilde{S}
factors through either

\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\Bigr[b_{\alpha}/a_{\alpha}\Bigr]
or
\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\Bigl[a_{\alpha}/b_{\alpha}\Bigr]
.
Hence, either
b=ha
or
a=hb
for the image
h\in\widetilde{S}
of
b_{\alpha}/a_{\alpha}
or
a_{\alpha}/b_{\alpha}
, which implies that
\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}
is a valuation ring.

Next, we show
\widetilde{S}\simeq S\times_{k(\mathfrak{p})}\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}
, where
k(\mathfrak{p})=S/\mathfrak{p}
is the fraction field of
\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}
(see
[
KM
, Proposition 3.3, “
\Rightarrow 4
”]
).
Since
S
is local and
\mathfrak{p}S
is its maximal ideal, we have
S=\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Then, it is enough to check that the map
\mathfrak{p}\to\mathfrak{p}S
is an isomorphism. The map
\widetilde{S}\to S
is injective by Remark
4.2
(1)
, therefore
\mathfrak{p}\to\mathfrak{p}S
is injective.
Let
x/h
in
\mathfrak{p}S
, with
x\in\mathfrak{p}
and
h\in\widetilde{S}\setminus\mathfrak{p}
.
There exists
\alpha\in A
such that
x,h
come from

x_{\alpha},h_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}
and
h_{\alpha}
is a unit in
S_{\alpha}
.
Then,
we have the v-étale covering
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,f}\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,x}\to\mathcal{T}
with

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,f}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\Bigl[\frac{x_{\alpha}}{h_{\alpha}}\Bigr])),\quad\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,x}=(\operatorname{Spec}(S_{\alpha}[1/x_{\alpha}]),\operatorname{Spec}(\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\Bigl[\frac{h_{\alpha}}{x_{\alpha}}\Bigr])).

As before, this implies that
\widetilde{S}_{\alpha}\to\widetilde{S}
factors through either

\widetilde{S}[x_{\alpha}/h_{\alpha}]
or
\widetilde{S}[h_{\alpha}/x_{\alpha}]
. In the latter case, there is
y\in\widetilde{S}
such that
yx=h
,
but this is impossible since
h\not\in\mathfrak{p}
. In the former case, there is
y\in\widetilde{S}
such that
yh=x
, hence
x/h=y\in\mathfrak{p}
, which implies that
\mathfrak{p}\to\mathfrak{p}S
is surjective.

Next, we show that
S
is henselian (see
[
KM
, Proposition 3.4]
). We follow the argument in
[
HS20
, Lemma 10.7]
.
Let
S\to B
be finite with
\operatorname{Spec}(B)
connected. Then,
B
is semilocal, and to show that
S
is henselian, it is enough to show that
B/\mathfrak{\mathfrak{p}}B
is local. Since it is a finite algebra over the field
S/\mathfrak{\mathfrak{p}}S
, it is enough to check that
\operatorname{Spec}(B/\mathfrak{\mathfrak{p}}B)
is connected. Let
\tilde{B}
be the integral closure of
\widetilde{S}
in
B
.
We claim

(1)

\tilde{B}
is local;

(2)

\mathfrak{p}B\subset\tilde{B}
;

(3)

\tilde{B}/\mathfrak{p}B
is the integral closure of
\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}\widetilde{S}
in
B/\mathfrak{p}B
.

Then
\tilde{B}/\mathfrak{p}B
is local by
(1)
,
(2)

and hence
\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{B}/\mathfrak{p}B)
is connected.
We conclude by
[
Sta16
,
Tag 03GO
]
that
\operatorname{Spec}(B/\mathfrak{p}B)
is connected. Indeed, if
B/\mathfrak{p}B=R_{1}\times R_{2}
, then
\tilde{B}/\mathfrak{p}B=\widetilde{R}_{1}\times\widetilde{R}_{2}
, with
\widetilde{R}_{i}
the integral closure of
\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}
in
R_{i}
.

Thus it remains to show the three claims above.
For
(1)
we note that
\widetilde{S}\to\tilde{B}
is integral and hence
\tilde{B}
is a filtered union of its subrings

\tilde{B}_{i}
finite over
\widetilde{S}
. Since
\tilde{B}_{i}\to\tilde{B}\to B
are injective the maps

\operatorname{Spec}(B)\to\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{B})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{B}_{i})
have dense images.
Therefore, as
\operatorname{Spec}(B)
is connected so are
\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{B})
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{B}_{i})
. Since
\widetilde{S}
is henselian,

\tilde{B}_{i}
is local henselian for all
i
. Since the maps
\tilde{B}_{i}\to\tilde{B}_{j}
are finite, they are local maps of henselian local rings.
Hence,
\tilde{B}
is local.

(2)
. Take
y\in B
and
m\in\mathfrak{p}
.
Since
B
is integral over
S
, we can write
y^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}y^{i}
, for
a_{i}\in S
, so that

(my)^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}m^{n-i}(my)^{i}.

As
a_{i}m^{n-i}\in\mathfrak{p}S=\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\widetilde{S}
, for
i\in[0,n-1]
, we get
my\in\tilde{B}
.

(3)
. If
\overline{x}\in B/\mathfrak{p}B
and
\overline{g}\in\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{p}[T]
is monic such that
\overline{g}(\overline{x})=0
,
then let
x\in B
and
g\in\widetilde{S}[T]
be lifts of
\overline{x}
and
\overline{g}
.
Then
g(x)\in\mathfrak{p}B\subset\tilde{B}
, in particular
f(x)
is integral over
\widetilde{S}
, so there exists
h(T)\in\widetilde{S}[T]
